# Chapter 7Blockchain Technology (BCT) for Cyber Defence and CybersecurityA Systematic Review

Chandra Sekhar Bhusal

DOI: [10.1201/9781003449515-7](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003449515-7)

## 7.1 Introduction

Cyber defence is a strategy or practice aimed at protecting information, systems, and networks from unwanted cyber manipulation through the implementation of various security procedures. The basic goal of cyber defence is to prevent, detect, and regulate immediate reactions to cyber-attacks and threats to protect infrastructure and information \[[1](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_1)\]. Cybersecurity is the practice of defending computers, servers, mobile devices, electronic systems, networks, and data from malicious attacks by implementing a defence strategy \[[2](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_2)\]. Blockchain technology (BCT) with its unique features has proved as an emerging technology in the fields of finance, supply chain, healthcare, manufacturing, and many more. In recent days, BCT has been gaining attention in the field of cyber defence due to its strong security characteristics \[[3](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_3)\]. Features like decentralization, consensus mechanisms, immutability, traceability, and privacy provide a solid foundation for cyber defence and data and information security in cyberspace \[[1](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_1)\].

For the first time, blockchain was introduced with the concept of cryptocurrency, when Satoshi Nakamoto defied Bitcoin in 2008. Bitcoin \[[4](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_4)\] is the most popular blockchain implementation and essentially a cryptocurrency implementation \[[5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5)\]. A blockchain is a decentralized, continuously growing list of records, called “blocks”, that are linked together in the chain through a process called mining. Each of these blocks contains a series of cryptographically verified transactions, and these blocks are linked in a chain that respects the chronological order of the transactions \[[5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5)–[7](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_7)\]. A blockchain is a distributed ledger that records events that occur in a system. This ledger is immutable, and its contents are validated by all system nodes – community-validated \[[5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5)\]. Nodes in the blockchain network add new blocks by broadcasting to other nodes, allowing the entire blockchain to be verified at any given time. A new block is added to the blockchain network when all other nodes on the network reach consensus. Blockchain follows different consensus mechanisms such as Proof of Work (PoW) and Proof of Stake (PoS) \[[7](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_7), [8](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_8)\].

[Figure 7.1](#chapter7.xhtml#fig7_1) \[[9](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_9)\] illustrates the structure of blocks in the blockchain network. Block is a data structure divided into the header and the body. Each block header contains the following information: hash of the previous block, hash of the current block, nonce, Merkle root, and other details \[[7](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_7), [8](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_8)\]. Each block contains the cryptographic hash of the previous block, so the blocks are connected to the previous block. The first block from which a blockchain is generated, i.e., block 0, is known as the “genesis block”. This means that the genesis block has no hash of the previous block \[[5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5), [8](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_8)\]. Time represents the digitally recorded moment when the block has been mined which is important in validating the transaction. Nonce are 4-byte fields that are only changeable in blocks of transactions which are used by miners until the right block hash is found. The Markle root is a 32-byte field that is built hierarchically by combining hashes of individual transactions in the block \[[5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5), [6](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_6), [8](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_8)\].

![Figure 7.1 Structure of blocks in blockchain.](images/fig7_1.jpg)

[Figure 7.1](#chapter7.xhtml#b7fig7_1) Structure of blocks in blockchain.

BCT has been a successful test case in many fields from finance, supply chain, healthcare, manufacturing, and much more due to its unique features of data immutable, distributed, decentralized, enhanced security, consensus mechanism, and transparency \[[1](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_1), [10](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_10)–[12](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_12)\]. In recent years cybersecurity experts have been exploring the new emerging technology that can counter the most advanced cyber threats. With the advancement of technologies, cyber threats are evolving, and attackers are using complex and complicated techniques that are challenging to prevent and counter. BCT has gained a lot of attention for securing and safeguarding our cyberspace. Due to its inbuilt features such as immutability decentralization, consensus mechanism, traceability, secure data storage, and privacy, blockchain is one of the most potential technologies that can provide a defence mechanism against sophisticated cyber-attacks. Blockchain offers a high level of data protection and integrity with the mechanism of access verification, authentication of transaction records, traceability, and maintaining privacy \[[1](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_1), [10](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_10), [12](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_12), [13](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_13)\].

Cybersecurity, being focused on how the cyber defence (approach employed to safeguard systems and the information and data they hold) is implemented, is not only concerned with the security of the information but is a broad concept that comprises various elements: network security, application security, end-user training, information security, operational security, and disaster recovery planning \[[14](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_14), [15](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_15)\]. Network security is a set of solutions that allow organizations to keep computer networks safe against hackers, organized attackers, and malware. It is designed to safeguard the network from unauthorized user access and no illegitimate activities inside the network. Application security is the use of hardware and software (such as antivirus programs, encryption, and firewalls) to safeguard the system from external dangers that may interfere with software applications. Information security guards against unauthorized access, disclosure, misuse, unauthorized alterations, and deletion of both physical and digital data. End-user training refers to educating the user about the safe and proper use of social media, text messages, email, passwords, and other critical components of the organization. Users are unpredictable aspects of cybersecurity as a human mistake is one of the primary errors that lead to breaches of an organization's information \[[15](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_15)\]. Disaster recovery planning is the planning that outlines how to continue working quickly and effectively after a disaster. Disaster recovery planning aims to safeguard an organization during unforeseen events. Its primary goals are to provide a sense of security, reduce the likelihood of delays, ensure that backup systems are reliable, and offer a clear standard for testing the preparedness plan \[[14](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_14)\]. Operational security refers to the processes and actions taken to protect information that can be used to infer critical data or insights about an organization's operations. It focuses on preventing unauthorized individuals, especially adversaries, from obtaining and exploiting this sensitive information. The principle is to identify and protect information that, if captured by malicious actors, could be used against the organization \[[14](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_14), [15](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_15)\].

## 7.2 Blockchain as Countermeasures in Cybersecurity

BCT has been explored as a countermeasure in the realm of cybersecurity for a variety of reasons. At its core, a blockchain is a decentralized and distributed digital ledger used to record transactions across many computers in a way that ensures the data can only be modified once it's been recorded, and even then, only after the modification is validated by consensus. Here's how blockchain can be utilized as a countermeasure in cybersecurity.

### 7.2.1 Blockchain for Data Integrity

Data integrity is defined as the process of maintaining and guaranteeing data's accuracy and consistency and protecting it from unauthorized user alteration or destruction. In a centralized system, all the information is managed at a single point of control by a central authority or system. The entire dataset can be jeopardized whenever the central point is compromised. Blockchain as an immutable, distributed ledger offers an innovative approach to maintaining data integrity. Focusing on data integrity, once data is recorded on a blockchain, altering it without the network's consensus is impossible. Each transaction in a block is cryptographically signed by its sender and its miner, and each block contains the hash of the previous block, which all participants in the blockchain network agree on. The idea is that the chain is meant to act as a shared truth \[[1](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_1), [5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5)\]. To change a single transaction on the blockchain, an attacker must change every subsequent block, solve the consensus challenge for that and subsequent blocks, and persuade more than half of the network members to accept the changed chain. Due to the hashing features and the amount of computing and electrical resources necessary to achieve this aim, a situation like that is nearly impossible \[[13](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_13), [16](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_16)\]. Also, the decentralized nature of the blockchain means that multiple independent nodes verify every transaction. This distributed consensus provides multiple points of verification, ensuring the accuracy and authenticity of every recorded transaction \[[9](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_9)\].

### 7.2.2 Blockchain for Non-Repudiation

Non-repudiation refers to the assurance that a party involved in a communication or transaction cannot deny the authenticity of their signature, the content of their message, or receipt of a message which is crucial to ensure accountability. Centralized systems achieve non-repudiation by use of various techniques like digital signature, timestamping, multi-factor authentications, and many more. There are inherent vulnerabilities in the centralized system like a single point of failure (SPoF), insider threats, lack of distributed verification, and limited transparency. Blockchain as a revolutionary technology has unique features to solve non-repudiation issues of the centralized system. The sender cannot ever refuse about order of transaction as every blockchain transaction has its sender's cryptographic signature and timestamped. This signature ensures that a specific transaction can be attributed to a particular user, and they can't later deny (repudiate) having initiated that transaction. The validity of the signature can be verified by anyone using the sender's public key, but it can only be created by someone who possesses the corresponding private key. Once a transaction has been validated and added to a block on the blockchain, it becomes nearly immutable. This means that it is extremely difficult for anyone to alter or delete a transaction once it has been recorded. Hence there is always persistent evidence of every transaction and its originator \[[5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5)\]. The blockchain structure consists of blocks that are chronologically linked together. Each block contains a cryptographic hash that refers to the preceding block. This ensures a continuous, unbroken chain of records that allows every action or transaction to be tracked and verified with confidence \[[1](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_1)\].

### 7.2.3 Blockchain for Availability

Availability refers to the ability of a system or service to be accessible and operational when required by users. The idea of availability is related to the concept of anti-SPoF \[[5](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_5)\]. Availability is a crucial factor in any system, especially in contexts where downtime can result in financial losses, decreased trust, or operational inefficiencies. In centralized systems, the concentration of resources and control relies on a singular or limited number of points, which can be more challenging in terms of availability due to a SPoF, scalability issues, distributed denial-of-service (DDoS), infrastructure failure, or backup failure. BCT inherently addresses this concern through its decentralized structure and design. Traditional centralized systems maintain, control, and validate the transactions. This centralized nature makes them vulnerable to fraud, corruption, and abuse and results in a SPoF. Blockchain unlike a centralized system operates on a decentralized network of nodes where operations/transactions are maintained by the participating nodes and each node keeps a copy of the entire blockchain ledger. This ensures that even if some nodes face issues, the system remains operational and still can provide service requests, and the whole system never falls. This approach makes the blockchain network available all the time and drastically reduces the vulnerability of the entire system going offline \[[3](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_3)\].

### 7.2.4 Blockchain for Privacy

The safeguarding of sensitive and private data against unauthorized access, use, disclosure, interruption, alteration, or destruction is referred to as privacy. A centralized system where the control of user data is with a single or limited number of points presents challenges and vulnerabilities such as a single point of compromise, insider threats, lack of user control, and many others. In a typical blockchain system, users transact using a combination of public and private keys. The public key is visible and linked to the transaction records, but the real identity of the user is unknown. Because of this pseudo-anonymity, only the owner of the private key can authorize a transaction, guaranteeing the privacy of individual acts. Blockchain users use pseudo-anonymous or anonymous addresses so that their identities are hidden. This allows them to retain a degree of control over their personal information and maintain their online activities private \[[7](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_7)\]. A pseudonymous address is a unique string of characters that is used as a user's digital identity on the blockchain. Such addresses are generated by cryptographic procedures, which are not connected directly to the real person's identity. So, tracking the activities of specific user to their actual identity in the blockchain is a real challenge \[[1](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_1)\].

### 7.2.5 Blockchain for Accountability

Accountability is the ability to trace and pinpoint individuals who have accessed or modified sensitive information. Accountability makes sure that people may be held accountable if they misuse data and that they are liable for their actions. It is crucial in cybersecurity to prevent online attacks and preserve stakeholder trust. In the traditional centralized system, accountability is maintained by implementing strict access control measures, monitoring system activity, and maintaining audit logs. However, these techniques are prone to cyber threats along with insider threats and malware attacks. BCT inherently promotes accountability by providing transparent, immutable, and verifiable records of transactions or data entries. All the transactions in the blockchain can be identified and traced to find unauthorized access attempts back to the source where every transaction is permanently recorded on an immutable ledger. All the transactions and data entries (access attempts and access logs) are maintained in the temper-proof ledger and verified by all the participating nodes making them accountable for their action \[[7](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_7), [17](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_17)\].

### 7.2.6 Blockchain for Secure Transaction

Centralized systems rely on a single entity to validate transactions, whereas decentralized blockchains use a consensus mechanism to agree upon the validity of transactions. These mechanisms ensure that all nodes in the network reach a consensus that enables to monitoring of malicious actions, anomalies, and false positives without the involvement of the central authority. Blockchain has emerged as a promising technology for ensuring secure transactions due to its inherent features like decentralization, immutability, transparency, and cryptographic security. Due to the blockchain's distributed nature, transactions take place over a network of nodes, and the lack of central authority eliminates the vulnerabilities associated with centralized systems \[[17](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_17)\]. Once a transaction has been added to a block and that block is uploaded to the blockchain, it becomes nearly impossible to modify. To successfully attack a decentralized blockchain, an attacker typically needs to control more than 50% of the network's computational power, making large networks extremely secure. This immutability protects transaction history's integrity, making fraudulent modification detectable and discouraging tampering attempts. All the transactions in a blockchain are safeguarded using cryptographic measures. The distributed ledger uses public key infrastructure (PKI) to protect communication, verify devices, authenticate configuration changes, and detect sensitive devices \[[9](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_9), [18](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_18)\].

## 7.3 BCT-Based Cybersecurity use Cases

BCT offers a novel approach to address various cybersecurity issues. Blockchain's decentralized, transparent, and tamper-evident nature makes it a potential solution for various cybersecurity concerns. Several companies leverage BCT for cybersecurity applications to enhance the trustworthiness, security, and integrity of their offerings. Blockchain is being used by these organizations in cybersecurity to provide unprecedented protection for their electronic assets. [Table 7.1](#chapter7.xhtml#tbl7_1) shows a few leading organizations in various fields from cryptocurrency, banking, and healthcare to Internet of Things (IoT) that are building BCT-based cybersecurity solutions.

### 7.3.1 Coinbase

Coinbase is a cryptocurrency exchange founded in 2012 in the United States by Brian Armstrong and Fred Ehrsam. The Coinbase app lets users purchase, trade, and exchange various cryptocurrencies on the open market. Customers who sign up for a Coinbase account can trade, sell, buy, withdraw, and deposit all types of cryptocurrencies \[[19](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_19), [20](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_20)\]. The private keys of Coinbase customers are saved locally on their devices, and users have total control over their cryptocurrencies, keys, and data. Coinbase uses smart contract-based encryption to provide safe crypto trading benefits \[[21](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_21), [22](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_22)\].

Coinbase's technology stack includes a variety of technologies. Some of these include Node.js, Ruby on Rails, Django, and Go. In 2020, the Coinbase mobile app was redesigned from scratch with React Native \[[23](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_23)\]. Cold and Hot Storage is one of the robust features of Coinbase where the majorities (estimated 98%) of Coinbase's customer funds are stored in cold wallets, which means they are offline and secure from online cyber threats. The hot wallets are also insured which means if there is a breach at Coinbase that results in a loss of client funds, the insurance coverage will pay to cover the loss. All information, including client data and wallet private keys, is encrypted both in transit and at rest \[[24](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_24)\].

### 7.3.2 MobileCoin

MobileCoin, developed by MobileCoin Inc, is a peer-to-peer cryptocurrency, which was founded in 2017 in California, United States by Josh Goldbard and Shane Glynn \[[19](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_19), [25](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_25)\]. It is a cryptocurrency designed with a focus on privacy, security, and ease of use on mobile devices. Its primary purpose is to integrate with mobile messaging apps, allowing for quick and secure peer-to-peer transactions \[[26](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_26)\]. The MobileCoin works with messaging apps like Facebook Messenger, WhatsApp, and Signal \[[19](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_19)\].

MobileCoin's mechanism is built on the Stellar Consensus Protocol (SCP), which allows for decentralized control, low latency, and flexible trust. For privacy, the MobileCoin's blockchain ledger is built on the CryptoNote ledger encryption protocol, which uses one-time recipient addresses to disguise the identity of all users’ transaction outputs (TxOuts). Also, MobileCoin utilizes Ring Confidential Transactions (RingCT) (features from Monero) that ensure that the transaction amounts are hidden which provides privacy by masking the sender and receiver addresses \[[25](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_25), [26](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_26)\].

MobileCoin has implemented zero-knowledge proof (ZKP) to ensure MobileCoin users can communicate securely, with each other and send and receive MobileCoin (MOB) tokens while preventing attackers from manipulating or learning about their transaction amounts. ZKPs ensure that no one other than you and not even the application's service provider can access your data.

### 7.3.3 Founders Bank

Founders Bank was a proposed project aiming to establish a community-owned, blockchain-based bank. It was backed by Binance, one of the largest cryptocurrency exchanges in the world. The main purpose of this project was to bridge the gap between traditional financial systems and the crypto ecosystem. Founders Bank will be decentralized with no central authority. Instead, it will be community-owned by all its customers \[[27](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_27)\].

The bank uses a decentralized storage system, a vast public ledger system, and encryption methods to ensure the secure trading of cryptocurrencies. The blockchain implementation in Founders Bank allows token-based ownership and a decentralized governance model where the token holders could have an important role in the bank's operation and decisions. With this project, the bank aims to seamlessly fait to crypto conversion and business accounts for crypto enterprises. They will use smart contracts to automate certain banking processes, enhance transparency, and reduce operational costs. The major advantage of blockchain implementation will be quicker and more cost-effective cross-border transactions \[[26](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_26), [27](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_27)\].

### 7.3.4 JPMorgan Chase

In 2016, JPMorgan created Quorum, a private blockchain based on the Ethereum architecture.

Quorum is an Ethereum-based distributed ledger protocol that has been modified to cater to the needs of the financial industry, emphasizing privacy and permissioned network functionalities \[[22](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_22)\].

Quorum is a private/permissioned blockchain based on the Ethereum protocol's official Go implementation. The basic idea of Quorum is to employ encryption to prevent anybody other than the parties involved in the transaction from accessing sensitive data. It consists of a single shared blockchain and a mix of smart contract software architecture. Quorum employs a majority voting mechanism known as QuorumChain, in which a subset of network nodes are granted the capacity to vote on blocks. Quorum introduces the idea of private transactions, which are only accessible to network users who are participating in the transaction. Quorum introduces the idea of private transactions, which are accessible only to network users participating in the transaction \[[28](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_28), [29](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_29)\].

### 7.3.5 Santander US

Santander was the first UK bank to use blockchain to securitize its international payments services. Customers can safely transfer funds across Santander accounts in Europe and South America thanks to the bank's BCT \[[21](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_21)\].

Santander has launched a foreign exchange service, One Pay FX, a blockchain-based international money transfer service for retail customers in various countries, using Ripple's xCurrent technology with this, the bank became the first bank to simultaneously launch a blockchain-based international payments solution for retail clients in different countries. Customers in the United Kingdom may download the One Pay FX app, connect using their standard Santander mobile banking credentials, and begin making payments. It integrates with Apple Pay, where users may securely confirm payments using Touch or Face ID \[[30](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_30)\]. Santander has invested in Securitize, a platform for issuing and managing digital assets on the blockchain \[[31](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_31)\]. Santander claims to be the first institution to use blockchain to manage all elements of a bond offering. The bank issued a $20 million bond on the public Ethereum blockchain, and one of its units acquired it, resulting in a complete on-chain settlement. This enables Santander to achieve the milestone of safely tokenizing the bond and registering it on the blockchain in a permissioned way \[[32](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_32)\].

### 7.3.6 Philips Healthcare

Philips Healthcare, based in Cambridge, Massachusetts, is using blockchain and artificial intelligence to develop a new and safe healthcare environment. In collaboration with hospitals throughout the world, the firm uses artificial intelligence to identify and evaluate all parts of the healthcare system, including operational, administrative, and medical data. The huge volumes of data collected are then secured via blockchain \[[21](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_21), [22](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_22), [33](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_33)\].

To develop an Ethereum-based blockchain application that may be used to maintain medical records, save patient data, or ease payments between healthcare providers, Philips Healthcare is collaborating with Cyberium and Gem Health Network \[[34](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_34)\]. Cyberium is a BCT that offers a set of tools that allow developers to easily design and deploy decentralized applications (dApps) on the Ethereum blockchain. Gem Health is a blockchain network for the worldwide community of organizations and individuals involved in the healthcare continuum \[[35](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_35)\].

### 7.3.7 Hashed Health

Hashed Health, based in Nashville, Tennessee, is a healthcare innovation organization committed to assisting the sector in the implementation of BCT. Hashed Health has developed safe digital blockchain networks with hundreds of healthcare organizations and businesses for internal communication and the sharing of patient data \[[19](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_19)\]. Hashed Health uses Hyperledger, the cross-industry blockchain that has significant influence in the financial, infrastructure, and healthcare sectors. Through the implementation of BCT, Hashed Health intends to ensure safe and secure access to patient data, create well-defined care teams that can communicate effectively create well-defined care plans for patients, and track critical supplies such as medicines \[[36](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_36)\].

Some of Hashed Health's blockchain implementations and initiatives include ProCredEx (Professional Credentials Exchange), Signal Stream, and Blockchain Consortium. ProCredEx, launched by Hashed Health, is a digital marketplace that uses blockchain to simplify and accelerate the process of sharing, confirming, and certifying professional credentials in healthcare. It is intended to eliminate the credentialing process’ duplication and inefficiencies \[[36](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_36)\]. Signal Stream is the first blockchain/distributed ledger technology (DLT) multi-stakeholder business management platform in healthcare, providing multi-system data collection and reporting capabilities to create a continuous source of information for secure, scalable, and confidential shared processes and contracts in healthcare \[[37](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_37)\]. Hashed Health created a consortium of healthcare organizations interested in researching and expanding blockchain solutions. Members of the consortium can work together to build blockchain-based solutions and benefit from one another's experiences \[[38](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_38)\].

### 7.3.8 MedicalChain

MedicalChain is a decentralized platform that uses BCT to securely store and manage health information, enabling interoperability, security, and patient-centricity in the management of electronic health records (EHRs) \[[21](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_21)\]. MedicalChain goal is to give patient authority over their medical data by allowing them to share the most comprehensive version of their record with every organization in their medical network. Patients have ultimate ownership over their data using MedicalChain. Patients control which entities have access to their medical records, and any modification or access to their data is auditable. To secure health data, the platform employs cryptographic technologies. Medical records are encrypted and stored in a decentralized manner, ensuring data is tamper-proof and accessible only to those with the appropriate permissions \[[39](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_39)\].

MedicalChain utilizes a dual blockchain structure: MedicalChain uses the Ethereum blockchain for a variety of applications, including identity verification, audit trail creation, and governance functions. The smart contract capabilities of Ethereum are critical for features that ensure only authorized access to medical information and Hyperledger Fabric is used for core management of health records. This enables scalable, secure data administration with sophisticated access control measures \[[39](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_39)\]. MedToken (MTN) is a token within the MedicalChain ecosystem. This token enables a variety of behaviours, such as rewarding patients to provide data for research or gaining access to various platform features.

### 7.3.9 HDAC

The Hyundai Digital Asset Currency (HDAC) is a platform to enable authentication, mapping, and machine-to-machine (M2M) communication by fusing BCT with IoT technology. A private blockchain is used by the HDAC platform to establish a straightforward transaction environment for M2M communications.

HDAC created its blockchain, apart from Ethereum or Bitcoin, called RIZON blockchain, which is an IoT-friendly blockchain, public/private blockchain. To boost transaction pace and volume, the Hdac system employs a dual chain structure, one public and one private. This implies that the platform may be used with both IoT devices and industrial networks. HDAC introduced a unique mechanism known as ePOW (elected PoW) as a consensus algorithm that aims to be more energy-efficient and provide a faster block generation time \[[40](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_40), [41](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_41)\]. The IoT contract, a smart contract related to IoT devices that communicate with the blockchain, is the fundamental component of the HDAC platform. Thing owners may describe tasks for their devices to do, as well as security and access-control restrictions, using IoT contracts. HDAC uses its token (HDAC) on its platform for a variety of purposes, including transaction fees and smart contract execution \[[41](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_41)\].

### 7.3.10 Xage Security

Xage Security, based in California, is a cybersecurity firm that has integrated BCT into its solutions, which are largely focused on the Industrial IoT (IIoT) and critical infrastructure. For example, when a new device is linked to an existing environment, the consensus is used to confirm its purpose or, if it is malicious, to exclude it from future communication. As a result, it may be viewed as a self-healing system that automatically changes the implementation of security policies directly on each edge device, allowing for more granular management of users and applications \[[42](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_42)\].

Xage introduced the blockchain-based “Security Fabric” which is a decentralized solution to provide complete security across a wide range of devices. This fabric distributes authentication and rules across nodes, ensuring that the entire system remains safe even if one node is hacked \[[43](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_43)\]. Supermajority consensus, a novel consensus process utilized by the security fabric blockchain, determines if a transaction is legitimate if most nodes approve it, send to assess the validity of a transaction – if most nodes accept it, it is valid. When many organizations share sensitive information, it permits supermajority conditional consensus. With an emphasis on IIoT, Xage Security has implemented the Zero Trust model into its security fabric. Zero Trust is a security model that relies on the core premise of “never trust, always verify”, regardless of whether the request originates within or outside the network. It is a departure from standard security models, which normally functions based on a secure internal network vs an untrusted external network \[[43](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_43), [44](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_44)\].

## 7.4 Challenges and Limitations

BCT, which has been recognized for its potential to revolutionize different industries, offers indisputable benefits such as enhanced transparency, traceability, and fewer intermediaries. Blockchain, being an emerging technology, is experiencing several challenges and issues. Here are some challenges that organizations may face when incorporating blockchain into their cybersecurity strategy: data privacy, scalability, policies and regulations, interoperability, energy consumption, and selfish mining.

### 7.4.1 Data Privacy

In the public blockchain, anyone can inspect the transaction history of any address or entity, which can inadvertently expose patterns or data related to user behaviour. While these transactions are linked to pseudonymous addresses rather than personal names, patterns might emerge with enough data analysis that may lead to de-anonymization, especially when paired with off-chain data. This is an issue for firms who wish to tightly manage what information is made public. Many of these privacy concerns can be mitigated with permissioned blockchain. A permissioned network, created by an enterprise blockchain platform, allows only trusted parties to participate in or observe transactions and vote on decisions \[[45](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_45), [46](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_46)\].

### 7.4.2 Scalability

Scalability, in the context of blockchain, refers to the capacity of a blockchain system to handle an increased volume of transactions and accommodate more users without compromising performance or security. While BCT provides a breakthrough approach to data integrity and decentralization, it has significant scaling challenges. The underlying architecture of many blockchain systems, particularly those of the first generation, such as Bitcoin, restricts the number of transactions the system can handle per second. This constraint arises from factors such as block size and frequency of block formation. To provide security and anonymity, every node in this system saves, processes, and retains transactions in a block. However, as the number of transactions rises, small- and medium-sized businesses struggle to accommodate an increasing number of transactions in a block. These increases may potentially hamper the validation process. These scalability issues have sparked a flurry of study and invention, to develop solutions that preserve blockchain's basic benefits while effectively tolerating higher transaction volumes \[[45](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_45)–[47](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_47)\].

### 7.4.3 Policies and Regulations

While BCT has the potential to disrupt many industries, it faces complex policy and regulatory constraints. As a new technology with numerous unknown and unanticipated risks and difficulties, blockchain is currently awaiting legal regulation adoption. Regulatory organizations are also concerned about ensuring that blockchain systems, particularly those that handle financial transactions or sensitive data, maintain acceptable security requirements to avoid hacks or data breaches \[[47](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_47)–[49](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_49)\]. Organizations are still attempting to figure out how the structure and complexity of blockchain fit into the developing data privacy, compliance, and regulatory landscape. The European General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) grants individuals the “right to be forgotten”, which allows them to request that their data be erased. This contradicts the immutability of blockchain. If personal data is directly kept on a public blockchain, it is hard to erase, posing a challenge to GDPR compliance \[[45](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_45)\].

### 7.4.4 Interoperability

In the context of blockchain, interoperability refers to the capacity of multiple blockchain systems to communicate, share information, and execute transactions across their respective networks in a seamless manner \[[50](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_50)\]. Interoperability is still a major hurdle in the emerging blockchain world. Despite its disruptive promise, blockchain has given rise to several networks, each with its own set of protocols, consensus processes, and cryptographic standards \[[51](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_51)\]. These isolated ecosystems often struggle to exchange and interact with one another, resulting in fragmented data and asset pools. Such constraints hinder the greater use of blockchains in scenarios that necessitate cross-chain interactions \[[52](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_52)\]. Open protocols, multichain frameworks, and algorithms are taking hold in blockchain, alleviating this problem. GS1 has established global standards for blockchain interoperability and is working with Microsoft and IBM to incorporate those standards into their business blockchain applications. The Enterprise Ethereum Alliance is also working to create commercial standards \[[45](#chapter7.xhtml#ref7_45)\]. Addressing interoperability is critical for realizing blockchain's full potential and creating a coherent and globally accessible decentralized digital future.

## 7.5 Conclusion

BCT has swiftly grown from a core component for Bitcoin transactions to a transformational tool with far-reaching ramifications for a variety of industries, including cyber defence and security. Traditional techniques of guaranteeing cybersecurity are occasionally inadequate as digital attacks become more complex. With its unique properties of decentralization, transparency, immutability, and cryptographic security, BCT has the potential to fill this need. Blockchain's decentralized nature eliminates the risks generally associated with centralized systems in the context of cyber protection. This decentralization not only spreads data over several nodes but also eliminates the possibility of a SPoF. As a result, enemies confront enormous difficulties when attempting to undermine the integrity or availability of data held within a blockchain. Furthermore, blockchain's immutability assures that once data is saved, it cannot be changed without leaving a trail. This is critical for tracking and authenticating digital actions, making fabrication or unauthorized modification impossible. Furthermore, the cryptographic methods built into blockchain systems ensure data security and integrity by allowing only authorized parties to view and alter the data. However, blockchain, like any other technology, is not a panacea. Scalability, energy usage, and interaction with current systems are all issues. Furthermore, while blockchain can dramatically improve cybersecurity, it should be seen as a component of a broader security plan rather than a stand-alone solution. Future research and applications will most likely concentrate on optimizing blockchain architecture for specific use cases in cybersecurity, building hybrid solutions, and guaranteeing seamless interaction with other upcoming technologies such as quantum computing or artificial intelligence. Furthermore, as regulatory environments develop, it will be critical to verify that blockchain solutions adhere to global standards and best practices. To summarize, BCT can reshape the paradigms of cyber defence and security. As cyber threats advance, the incorporation of blockchain techniques, solutions, and applications will be crucial in developing the next generation of cybersecurity countermeasures. This innovative technique may very well serve as the foundation for the future of cyber resiliency.

# Chapter 8webCyberBlockCybersecurity and Cyber Ethics via Blockchain Technology – Need for Web Security, Software Practices, and End-User Cyber Education

Devendra Chapagain, Naresh Kshetri, and Vikas Kumar Sihag

DOI: [10.1201/9781003449515-8](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003449515-8)

## 8.1 Introduction

In modern times, knowledge is highly prized and considered to be one of the most valuable assets. A majority of the world's population, approximately 63.5%, uses the internet and studies reveal that a significant 95% of cybersecurity breaches occur due to human mistakes \[[1](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_1)\]. Using the internet safely requires competent individuals who possess knowledge of potential threats, security protocols, and the ability to operate secure systems.

Teaching fundamental abilities like creating secure passwords, recognizing phishing scams, multi-layer verification steps, secure browsing, privacy setting of social media accounts, avoiding public Wi-Fi, and comprehending how data is obtained and a digital identity is monitored online can significantly increase a person's safety and cybersecurity. Similar to how it is exceedingly unsafe to operate a vehicle without knowledge of traffic laws and regulations, it is also incredibly dangerous to use the internet without understanding fundamental information security concepts. Unfortunately, even today, many IT graduates lack adequate information security knowledge and skills, which is a loophole in the education system and a concern for organizations as it can lead to security breaches. It is important for academic institutions to include information security in their curriculum.

IT graduates possess excellent abilities in programming and IT design; however, if they lack security skills, their IT solutions will inevitably be susceptible to vulnerabilities \[[2](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_2)\]. Although there are significant efforts in the IT industry to develop secure applications and software, there is very little effort to educate general internet users, including students, on how to use the internet safely. Activities such as online shopping and electronic payments, as well as the prevalent habit of young individuals spending most of their time with their mobile phones and laptops, have become the norm. The increasing number of people in Nepal who have access to the internet has presented the government of Nepal and the country's corporate sector with a number of challenging problems to solve. There are many kinds of cybercrime that have been reported in Nepal. Some of them are ransomware attacks, phishing scams, privacy leaks, and problems with social media like bullying, identity theft, pornography involving kids, and spreading false information.

Even if software or apps are approved to meet certain security standards, like International Standard Organization (ISO) standards, they could still have security problems. Security standards give guidelines and best practices for security, but they don't promise that a product will be completely safe. Security is an ongoing process, and even after a piece of software has been approved to meet a security standard, new holes can be found in it. A software application's security can also be broken by human mistake or on purpose by people who want to do harm. Because of this, it's important to update and keep software regularly to fix any known security flaws and to use extra security measures, like firewalls and antivirus software, to protect against possible threats.

## 8.2 Literature Review

In \[[3](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_3)\], it is analyzed that the weakest component of a technical system is people. In laying down the groundwork for the idea of human-centered cybersecurity, he chose the 3Us (user, usage, and usability) as the three parts of cybersecurity to think about when designing, implementing, and evaluating security.

To respond appropriately to cyberattacks in this environment, users need to be highly aware of their surroundings and the intentions of attackers. This allows them to quickly adapt and even anticipate unexpected attacks. Users need to consider a wide range of factors, such as the subject line and sender name of an email, their own knowledge of the sender, and whether the email is relevant to their current situation. They should also be able to go beyond this basic understanding to try to determine the attacker's intent and the potential cybersecurity consequences of clicking on the email. In other words, users need to be able to think critically about cyberattacks and make informed decisions about how to respond. This requires a deep understanding of the cyber threat landscape and the different types of attacks that can occur. Here is an example of how situational and intentional awareness can be used to respond to a cyberattack.

Imagine that you receive an email from someone claiming to be from your bank. The email subject line is “Urgent security alert” and the body of the email says that your account has been compromised and that you need to click on a link to reset your password. If you are not aware of the latest phishing scams, you might be tricked into clicking on the link. However, if you are aware of the threat of phishing emails, you will be more likely to be suspicious of this email. You might ask yourself the following questions:

*   Do I hold an account with this bank or institution?
    
*   Am I familiar with the person who sent the email?
    
*   Is the subject of this email related to any current events in my life?
    
*   Are there any other indications that this email might be untrustworthy?
    
*   Is there anything unusual about the timing of this email?
    
*   Is there any urgency or threat implied in the content of this email?
    
*   Has someone else in my network received an email like this?
    
*   Does this email encourage me to take action immediately without giving me time to think?
    
*   Have I received similar emails in the past, or do other people report similar emails as phishing or spam?
    
*   Does the email contain any spelling or grammar errors?
    

If you answer no to any of these questions, you should not click on the link in the email. Instead, you should contact your bank directly to verify the authenticity of the email. By being aware of your surroundings and the intentions of attackers, you can protect yourself from cyberattacks.

From a safety standpoint, \[[3](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_3)\] examine spam on social media Twitter or traffic samples from networks. Cybersecurity analysis relies heavily on data, and Twitter is a major source of that data. This survey presents a new data-driven cybersecurity research approach and reviews its use in social and internet traffic analysis. It highlights the close link between data, model, and methodology in current Twitter spam detection and IP traffic classification research. They discussed huge traffic data, subject expertise, and research methods.

Hackers are constantly ready to obtain personally identifiable information, commit crimes, or expose sensitive and helpful knowledge.

Attacks on national and international computer networks are a problem in a number of nations. Constant crises, the potential for a worldwide pandemic, and the need to have everything ready to go online all highlight the need for cyber-defense and cyber intelligence. Some form of cyber ethics and global cyber rules are required to guide some of the outstanding activities undertaken by governments of underdeveloped and developing countries \[[4](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_4)\].

In her 2015 article “Cyber-Attacks – Trends, Patterns and Security Countermeasures,” Andreea Bendovschi examines the ever-changing world of cybercrime and the rise of attacks in internet-dependent societies. The report discusses man-in-the-middle assaults, distributed denial-of-service (DDoS) attacks, ransomware, and social engineering and its effects on individuals and enterprises. After analysing over 15 million attacks, the research found a correlation between industries targeted and attack types, emphasizing the need for global cyber awareness. Bendovschi advises organizations to regularly assess risks, maintain healthy IT infrastructures, implement strong authentication, and invest in independent security evaluations to combat these dangers. The article emphasizes the necessity of cybersecurity in a tech-driven environment \[[5](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_5)\].

It is expected that there will be approximately 30.9 billion devices connected to each other via the Internet of Things (IoT) by the year 2025 \[[6](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_6)\]. It's clear from the results that hackers are constantly coming up with new ways to abuse systems, software, and information. An intriguing finding of the study is that less than half of security breaches can be traced back to criminal intent. Instead, the reasons are evenly distributed among three categories: criminal intent, **human error**, and system weaknesses. The findings highlight the reality that the success of an attack is due to a combination of factors, including the attacker's knowledge and ability as well as vulnerabilities on the victim's side, such as poorly written code, **human error,** and a **lack of safeguards to protect sensitive data**.

## 8.3 Current Scenario of Cybersecurity after Covid

The COVID-19 virus has had a big effect on cybersecurity, bringing about both new problems and changes to old trends. Cybercriminals have exploited company and organization weaknesses caused by the rapid transition to remote employment. Thus, ransomware, data breaches, and phishing schemes have increased in recent years.

Cyberattacks have grown a lot in the past few years, making them a bigger problem for both people and businesses. The COVID-19 pandemic made this problem worse because hackers could now target almost anyone. Due to bad internet security practices, many people have been hurt. During the pandemic, lockdowns made it easy for hackers to get into people's data. As a result, cybercriminals began to target more and more areas, such as banks, government, and non-governmental organizations \[[7](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_7)\]. In 2020, there were many data breaches in the United States, affecting a large number of people. A study by the Identity Theft Resource Center found that from September 2020 to September 2021, there were even more data breaches than the previous year. This shows a growing trend of data breaches from 2016 to 2021.

During the COVID-19 pandemic, there were more cases of certain hacking problems. The common types of cyberattacks that happened during this time are malware, hacking, ransomware, DDoS attacks, harmful domains, malicious browsing apps, denial-of-service (DoS) attacks, malicious mobile apps, spam emails, malicious posts on social media, business email compromise, advanced persistent threats (APTs), botnet attacks, and hacking.

The growing hazard of cyberattacks is highlighted in a report titled “Cybercrime in a Pandemic World: The Impact of COVID-19” by McAfee Enterprise and FireEye. It reveals that 81% of global organizations saw a rise in cyber threats during the pandemic, and 79% faced downtime due to cyber incidents during peak times. With the holiday season approaching, sectors like supply chain, e-commerce, retail, and travel are at increased risk due to higher consumer and business activity, putting business, employee, and consumer data in jeopardy. This underscores the urgent need for organizations to enhance their cybersecurity defenses \[[8](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_8)\].

In the past five years, the IC3 got around 652,000 complaints each year. People from all over the world reported these complaints about various internet scams. The chart below shows the yearly and total numbers of complaints and losses from 2018 to 2022. During these years, the IC3 got a total of 3.26 million complaints, and the victims lost a whopping $27.6 billion \[[9](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_9)\].

According to the Federal Bureau of Investigation Elder Report Fraud Report 2022, the number of cybercrime victims changes every year, showing a range of effects. Non-payment/non-delivery became the most common cybercrime in 2022, affecting 7,985 people. In 2021, personal data breach was the most common cybercrime, affecting 6,189 people. On the other hand, extortion was the biggest threat in 2020, with 23,100 victims. Crimes Against Children had the fewest number of victims in 2022, with 84, while threats of violence had the most victims, with 719, in 2021. In 2020, Crimes Against Children again came in last, but this time there were only 58 victims. Employment-related cybercrimes have always been in the middle, with 1,286, 1,408, and 1,867 victims in 2022, 2021, and 2020, respectively. These numbers show how online threats are always changing and have different effects on different people and on society as a whole \[[10](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_10)\].

## 8.4 Need of Cyber Education and Secure Web Habits

Although the internet provides significant convenience to people, it has the potential to cause harm as a result of their limited understanding of online risks. Skills in cybersecurity are more important in the modern world, and this is especially true for the younger generation, which is often the first to adopt new technology and consume new forms of media online \[[11](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_11)\]. Because of the increasing reliance on technology and the ubiquity of online risks, cyber education, and secure browsing habits are vital in today's digital age (see [Figure 8.1](#chapter8.xhtml#fig8_1)).

![Figure 8.1 Cyber ecosystem and the role of cyber schooling.](images/fig8_1.jpg)

[Figure 8.1](#chapter8.xhtml#b8fig8_1) Cyber ecosystem and the role of cyber schooling.

It is very common for almost every user to use an email address for identity in almost every website that requires login. Using an email address as an ID, on the other hand, poses serious security and privacy risks \[[12](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_12)\]. When an email address is used as a form of identification, however, significant security and privacy issues are introduced. Significant privacy concerns are raised by the fact that an individual's email address may be disclosed to the public. In fact, the author advised against using the same email address when establishing a website account. For added security, it should generate its own logon credentials. Identity theft is more likely to occur if you use the same email address to identify yourself on multiple websites.

Daisuke Miyamoto mentioned in his article \[[13](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_13)\] that users fall for phishing because they are unaware about the inner workings of URLs, domain names, and security credentials. To ensure that users only visit secure websites, he also proposed a piece of software that can be added to a web browser to work with an eye-tracking device. The software would be able to detect when the user is looking at the address bar, which is the part of the browser that shows the website address that the user is currently visiting. This could be useful for a number of reasons. For example, it could be used to prevent users from accidentally clicking on malicious links. It could also be used to improve the user experience by making it easier for users to navigate to the websites they want to visit.

## 8.5 Blockchain and Security Measures

Blockchain can be used in many sectors if utilized. One use of blockchain can be the secure software practices in online transactions. Blockchain is a highly evolved digital network that has gained significant traction in recent years. This technology is designed to create a decentralized and secure network of computers that work together to verify and validate transactions or data, making it an ideal solution for securing digital assets and information. By using cryptographic techniques, blockchain technology ensures that data and transactions remain tamper-proof and transparent, which enhances the trust and reliability of digital assets. As a result, blockchain technology has become an attractive option for organizations seeking robust security measures in various domains, including cybersecurity.

There may be numerous positive outcomes from implementing blockchain-based cybersecurity solutions. Some examples of these advantages are,

Decentralization:

Blockchain is a decentralized system that operates on a peer-to-peer network, making it challenging for attackers to initiate DDoS attacks. Since blockchain operates without a central authority, there is no single point of failure that might bring down the network.

Immutable Record Keeping:

Due to blockchain's immutable record of all transactions and data, fraudulent behaviors are easier to discover and prevent.

Transparency:

Blockchain technology provides transparency, enabling network participants to view all network transactions and activity. This facilitates the identification of malicious behavior and security vulnerabilities.

Authentication:

Blockchain technology provides a secure method for authentication, thereby reducing the risk of identity theft and other and other forms of cybercrime.

Blockchain for Cybersecurity:

The invention of blockchain technology is an absolutely ground-breaking advancement in the field of technology. It has been effective in implementing a comprehensive security policy that possesses the potential to revolutionize many different industries. Blockchain technology protects data from tampering and breaches of confidentiality by virtue of its decentralized nature, immutability, and the use of cryptographic algorithms \[[16](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_16)\]. This forward-thinking strategy does away with the necessity of using conventional middlemen, which in turn lowers the likelihood of fraudulent activity, illegal access, and manipulated data. Individuals and businesses now have the ability to conduct safe transactions, share sensitive information, and develop trust in a manner that is both transparent and efficient.

Blockchain technology has the potential to increase the safety of digital data and information. By utilizing its immutable nature and high level of trustworthiness, blockchain serves as a robust mechanism for safeguarding associated data. It provides a reliable means of protecting information, making it an effective tool for enhancing overall data security in the digital realm \[[14](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_14)\]. In the blockchain system, any modification, no matter how small, in a single piece of data triggers a series of subsequent changes throughout the entire chain. This mechanism ensures that any tampering or alteration of the stored data is immediately detectable. By using a secure hash algorithm, the blockchain encrypts data while it is being calculated. This algorithm possesses a valuable feature of enhancing data security. Moreover, it is designed to be user-friendly, simplifying the process of utilizing blockchain technology for encryption purposes.

The blockchain employs a unique technical mechanism to mitigate network attacks, thereby preventing unauthorized access to sensitive information by potential attackers. Hence, the distinctive feature of the blockchain technology can serve as a deterrent against malicious attacks and enhance the overall security of the network to a certain degree. The technical mechanism of blockchain generally results in a high level of security, thus highlighting the importance of prioritizing security concerns when utilizing blockchain technology for secure online browsing \[[15](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_15)\].

Using blockchain to enhance security is not a simple solution because it requires a lot of resources, which can lead to increased processing time and slower communication \[[16](#chapter8.xhtml#ref8_16)\]. Therefore, even when we make use of the most advanced technology, there will always be a requirement for human interaction. Therefore, having the ability to use the technology while also having a fundamental understanding of its security is necessary.

## 8.6 Conclusion and Future Scope

The report effectively exposes human mistakes as a prominent contributing factor in cybersecurity breaches, underscoring the urgent necessity for comprehensive cyber education initiatives. The proposition to incorporate cybersecurity education into academic curriculum and industry training is justified, as it aims to address a significant deficiency in knowledge that may accidentally result in the development of susceptible IT systems.

Most people don't understand the different types of attacks, how they work, and what could happen as a result. This could make it harder to protect information security.

Those countries that are more successful tend to have an office that is accountable for making sure that all of their inhabitants, from kindergarteners to retirees, receive the necessary education regarding the dangers of cyberspace. The nation of Singapore, which holds the second spot in the index, is responsible for maintaining the integrity and safety of its cyberspace by establishing the Cyber Security Agency of Singapore. Education is a priority in this country, in large part because of the widespread use of the internet and the vulnerability of its central financial district to assault. Its cyber-wellness courses span various grades, and its primary focus is on social and practical safety advice, such as how to recognize and respond appropriately to cyberbullying. This strategy of educating students about cyber risk is applicable in other countries as well.

# Chapter 9A Study on Machine Learning and Blockchain-Based Techniques for Transparent VNF Testing for 5G and B5G Networks

Panchanan Nath and Purnendu Shekhar Pandey

DOI: [10.1201/9781003449515-9](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003449515-9)

## 9.1 Introduction

Mobile networks have significantly evolved to meet market demands and user expectations, as shown in [Figure 9.1](#chapter9.xhtml#fig9_1). The deployment of fifth-generation (5G) mobile networks represents a profound shift in communication between humans and machines, with a strong focus on connectivity and interaction. Significantly, 5G is designed to cater to three primary traffic classes: eMBB, URLLC, and mMTC \[[1](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_1), [2](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_2)\]. These classes are customized to meet a wide range of personalized requirements, serving the needs of individuals and various network stakeholders, including industry verticals, MVNOs, and OTT service providers \[[3](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_3)\]. A variety of cutting-edge technologies, such as SDN, NFV, MEC, Network Slicing (NS), CC, MIMO, and NOMA, are also propelling the development of the 5G ecosystem \[[4](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_4), [5](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_5)\].

![Figure 9.1 Evaluation of networks with respect to market demands \[6\].](images/fig9_1.jpg)

[Figure 9.1](#chapter9.xhtml#b9fig9_1) Evaluation of networks with respect to market demands \[[6](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_6)\].

Revolutionary improvements in terms of data transfer bandwidth, low latency, enormous device connection, and seamless user experiences have been made possible by the ongoing growth of communication networks, particularly the 5G and beyond fifth-generation (B5G) networks \[[7](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_7)\]. The security and transparency of virtual network function (VNF) testing have become more vital in the constantly evolving 5G network environment as a result of these networks’ rapid evolution \[[8](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_8)\]. Modern methodologies that make use of machine learning (ML) algorithms and blockchain technology are required to enhance VNF testing in 5G and B5G networks because traditional testing techniques frequently lack transparency and are possibly unable to effectively deal with new security problems.

Increased security and process transparency are only two benefits of using ML and blockchain-based technologies in VNF testing. Evaluation of VNF performance, detection of anomalies, and identification of potential vulnerabilities are all achievable through the application of ML methods \[[9](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_9), [10](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_10)\]. By employing ML algorithms, VNF testing may proactively discover and minimize weaknesses in security, assuring the overall robustness of the network infrastructure \[[11](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_11)\]. These ML-based solutions enable early intervention and exact security risk assessments, which supports more trustworthy testing practices.

Additionally, using blockchain technology for VNF testing improves the process’ transparency and the auditability \[[12](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_12)\]. There is a high level of confidence in the test results while blockchain offers a distributed and immutable ledger that enables stakeholders to independently validate and trust the test results \[[13](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_13)\]. By combining ML and blockchain-based approaches, VNF performance and security can be monitored, analyzed, and validated in real time \[[14](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_14)\]. This strategy makes it easier for multiple organizations to effectively share test results, encouraging collaboration and facilitating thorough security assessments \[[15](#chapter9.xhtml#ref9_15)\].

This chapter contributes to the identification of potential challenges such as standardization efforts in VNF testing. It focuses on the advancements made in ML and blockchain-based techniques and their implications for ensuring secure and transparent VNF testing in 5G and B5G networks. The integration of ML algorithms enhances testing accuracy and security, while blockchain technology ensures transparency and accountability. Furthermore, future research directions can focus on optimizing ML algorithms for VNF testing, exploring novel blockchain-based solutions, and addressing standardization challenges to foster interoperability and consistency in VNF testing methodologies.
